href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Conservatism_sidebar" title="Template:Conservatism template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>George Canning</b> <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:85%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Fellow_of_the_Royal_Society" title="Fellow of the Royal Society">FRS</a></span> (11 April 1770&#160;&#8211;&#32;8 August 1827) was a <a href="/wiki/British_Tory" class="mw-redirect" title="British Tory">British Tory</a> statesman. He held various senior cabinet positions under numerous prime ministers, including two important terms as Foreign Secretary, finally becoming <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Prime Minister of the United Kingdom">Prime Minister of the United Kingdom</a> for the last 119 days of his life, from April to August 1827.
</p><p>The son of an actress and a failed businessman and lawyer, Canning was supported financially by his uncle, Stratford Canning, which allowed him to attend <a href="/wiki/Eton_College" title="Eton College">Eton College</a> and <a href="/wiki/Christ_Church,_Oxford" title="Christ Church, Oxford">Christ Church, Oxford</a>. Canning entered politics in 1793 and rose rapidly. He was <a href="/wiki/Paymaster_of_the_Forces" title="Paymaster of the Forces">Paymaster of the Forces</a> (1800–1801) and <a href="/wiki/Treasurer_of_the_Navy" title="Treasurer of the Navy">Treasurer of the Navy</a> (1804–1806) under <a href="/wiki/William_Pitt_the_Younger" title="William Pitt the Younger">William Pitt the Younger</a>. Canning was <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Foreign_Affairs_(UK)" class="mw-redirect" title="Secretary of State for Foreign Affairs (UK)">Foreign Secretary</a> (1807–1809) under the <a href="/wiki/William_Cavendish-Bentinck,_3rd_Duke_of_Portland" title="William Cavendish-Bentinck, 3rd Duke of Portland">Duke of Portland</a>. Canning was the dominant figure in the cabinet and directed the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Copenhagen_(1807)" title="Battle of Copenhagen (1807)">Battle of Copenhagen</a>, the seizure of the Danish fleet in 1807 to assure Britain's naval supremacy over <a href="/wiki/Napoleon" title="Napoleon">Napoleon</a>. In 1809, he was wounded in a duel with his rival <a href="/wiki/Robert_Stewart,_Viscount_Castlereagh" title="Robert Stewart, Viscount Castlereagh">Lord Castlereagh</a> and was shortly thereafter passed over as a successor to the Duke of Portland in favour of <a href="/wiki/Spencer_Perceval" title="Spencer Perceval">Spencer Perceval</a>. He rejected overtures to serve as Foreign Secretary again because of Castlereagh's presence in Perceval's Cabinet and he remained out of high office until after Perceval was assassinated in 1812.
</p><p>Canning served under the new Prime Minister the <a href="/wiki/Robert_Jenkinson,_2nd_Earl_of_Liverpool" title="Robert Jenkinson, 2nd Earl of Liverpool">Earl of Liverpool</a> as <a href="/wiki/British_Ambassador_to_Portugal" class="mw-redirect" title="British Ambassador to Portugal">British Ambassador to Portugal</a> (1814–1816), <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_Board_of_Control" title="President of the Board of Control">President of the Board of Control</a> (1816–1821) and Foreign Secretary and <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_House_of_Commons" title="Leader of the House of Commons">Leader of the House of Commons</a> (1822–1827). King George IV disliked Canning and there were efforts to frustrate his foreign policies. Canning gained much public support for his policies. The historian <a href="/wiki/Paul_Hayes_(historian)" title="Paul Hayes (historian)">Paul Hayes</a> argues that he achieved several diplomatic coups in relations with Spain and Portugal, by helping to guarantee the independence of the American colonies of <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_colonization_of_the_Americas" title="Portuguese colonization of the Americas">Portugal</a> and <a href="/wiki/Spanish_colonization_of_the_Americas" title="Spanish colonization of the Americas">Spain</a>. His policies ensured a major trading advantage for British merchants and supported the American <a href="/wiki/Monroe_Doctrine" title="Monroe Doctrine">Monroe Doctrine</a>. The historian <a href="/wiki/G._M._Trevelyan" title="G. M. Trevelyan">G. M. Trevelyan</a> wrote
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>For five years England had been guided by the genius of Canning, and seldom have so much brilliancy and so much wisdom combined to produce such happy results. The constitutional medium through which that genius worked was the loyal friendship of the prime minister, Lord Liverpool.<sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>When Lord Liverpool resigned in April 1827, Canning was chosen to succeed him as Prime Minister, ahead of <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington" title="Arthur Wellesley, 1st Duke of Wellington">the Duke of Wellington</a> and Sir <a href="/wiki/Robert_Peel" title="Robert Peel">Robert Peel</a>. Both of them declined to serve under Canning and the Tories split between Peel and Wellington's <a href="/wiki/Ultra-Tories" title="Ultra-Tories">Ultra-Tories</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Canningites" class="mw-redirect" title="Canningites">Canningites</a>. Canning then invited several <a href="/wiki/Whigs_(British_political_party)" title="Whigs (British political party)">Whigs</a> to join his cabinet. His health collapsed, and he died of pneumonia at Chiswick House on 8 August 1827, whilst still in office. His tenure of 119 days in office made him the <a href="/wiki/List_of_prime_ministers_of_the_United_Kingdom_by_length_of_tenure" title="List of prime ministers of the United Kingdom by length of tenure">shortest-serving prime minister in history</a>, a title he held for 195 years until <a href="/wiki/Liz_Truss" title="Liz Truss">Liz Truss</a>' premiership in 2022, which lasted 49 days.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=George_Canning&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Canning was born into an <a href="/wiki/Anglo-Irish" class="mw-redirect" title="Anglo-Irish">Anglo-Irish</a> family at his parents' home in Queen Anne Street, <a href="/wiki/Marylebone" title="Marylebone">Marylebone</a>, London. Canning described himself as "an Irishman born in London".<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> His father, <a href="/wiki/George_Canning_(writer)" title="George Canning (writer)">George Canning</a><sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> of <a href="/wiki/Garvagh" title="Garvagh">Garvagh</a>, <a href="/wiki/County_Londonderry" title="County Londonderry">County Londonderry</a>, in <a href="/wiki/Ulster" title="Ulster">Ulster</a>, the northern <a href="/wiki/Provinces_of_Ireland" title="Provinces of Ireland">province</a> in <a href="/wiki/Ireland" title="Ireland">Ireland</a>, was a gentleman of limited means, a failed wine merchant and lawyer, who renounced his right to inherit the family estate in exchange for payment of his substantial debts. George Sr. eventually abandoned the family and died in poverty on 11 April 1771, his son's first birthday, in London. Canning's mother, <a href="/wiki/Mary_Ann_Costello" title="Mary Ann Costello">Mary Ann Costello</a>, who was also <a href="/wiki/Irish_people" title="Irish people">Irish</a>, took work as a stage actress, a profession not considered respectable at the time.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> Indeed, when in 1827 it looked as if Canning would become Prime Minister, <a href="/wiki/Charles_Grey,_2nd_Earl_Grey" title="Charles Grey, 2nd Earl Grey">Lord Grey</a> remarked that "the son of an actress is, <i>ipso facto</i>, disqualified from becoming Prime Minister".<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Because Canning showed unusual intelligence and promise at an early age, family friends persuaded his uncle, London merchant Stratford Canning (father of the diplomat <a href="/wiki/Stratford_Canning,_1st_Viscount_Stratford_de_Redcliffe" title="Stratford Canning, 1st Viscount Stratford de Redcliffe">Stratford Canning</a>), to become his nephew's guardian. George Canning grew up with his cousins at the home of his uncle, who provided him with an income and an education. Stratford Canning's financial support allowed the young Canning to study at <a href="/wiki/Hyde_Abbey_School" title="Hyde Abbey School">Hyde Abbey School</a>, <a href="/wiki/Eton_College" title="Eton College">Eton College</a> and <a href="/wiki/Christ_Church,_Oxford" title="Christ Church, Oxford">Christ Church, Oxford</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> Canning came out top of the school at Eton and left at the age of seventeen.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> His time at Eton has been described as "a triumph almost without parallel. He proved a brilliant <a href="/wiki/Classics" title="Classics">classicist</a>, came top of the school, and excelled at public orations".<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_2-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Canning struck up friendships with the future <a href="/wiki/Robert_Jenkinson,_2nd_Earl_of_Liverpool" title="Robert Jenkinson, 2nd Earl of Liverpool">Lord Liverpool</a> as well as with <a href="/wiki/Granville_Leveson-Gower,_1st_Earl_Granville" title="Granville Leveson-Gower, 1st Earl Granville">Granville Leveson-Gower</a> and <a href="/wiki/John_Hookham_Frere" title="John Hookham Frere">John Hookham Frere</a>. In 1789 he won a prize for his Latin poem <i>The Pilgrimage to Mecca</i> which he recited in Oxford Theatre. Canning began practising law after receiving his BA from Oxford in the summer of 1791, but he wished to enter politics.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_2-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Entry_into_politics">Entry into politics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=George_Canning&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Entry into politics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Stratford Canning was a Whig and would introduce his nephew in the 1780s to prominent <a href="/wiki/British_Whig_Party" class="mw-redirect" title="British Whig Party">Whigs</a> such as <a href="/wiki/Charles_James_Fox" title="Charles James Fox">Charles James Fox</a>, <a href="/wiki/Edmund_Burke" title="Edmund Burke">Edmund Burke</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Richard_Brinsley_Sheridan" title="Richard Brinsley Sheridan">Richard Brinsley Sheridan</a>. George Canning's friendship with Sheridan would last for the remainder of Sheridan's life.
</p><p>George Canning's impoverished background and limited financial resources, however, made unlikely a bright political future in a Whig party whose political ranks were led mostly by members of the wealthy landed aristocracy in league with the newly rich industrialist classes. Regardless, along with Whigs such as Burke, Canning himself would become considerably more conservative in the early 1790s after witnessing the excessive radicalism of the <a href="/wiki/French_Revolution" title="French Revolution">French Revolution</a>. "The political reaction which then followed swept the young man to the opposite extreme; and his vehemence for monarchy and the Tories gave point to a Whig sarcasm,—that men had often turned their coats, but this was the first time a boy had turned his jacket."<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>So when Canning decided to enter politics he sought and received the patronage of the leader of the "Tory"<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> group, <a href="/wiki/William_Pitt_the_Younger" title="William Pitt the Younger">William Pitt the Younger</a>. In 1793, thanks to the help of Pitt, Canning became a member of parliament for <a href="/wiki/Newtown_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Newtown (UK Parliament constituency)">Newtown</a> on the <a href="/wiki/Isle_of_Wight" title="Isle of Wight">Isle of Wight</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Rotten_borough" class="mw-redirect" title="Rotten borough">rotten borough</a>. In 1796, he changed seats to a different rotten borough, <a href="/wiki/Wendover_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Wendover (UK Parliament constituency)">Wendover</a> in Buckinghamshire. He was elected to represent several <a href="/wiki/Constituency" class="mw-redirect" title="Constituency">constituencies</a> during his parliamentary career.
</p><p>Canning rose quickly in British politics as an effective orator and writer. His speeches in Parliament as well as his essays gave the followers of Pitt a rhetorical power they had previously lacked. Canning's skills saw him gain leverage within the Pittite faction that allowed him influence over its policies along with repeated promotions in the Cabinet. Over time, Canning became a prominent public speaker as well, and was one of the first politicians to campaign heavily in the country.
</p><p>As a result of his charisma and promise, Canning early on drew to himself a circle of supporters who would become known as the <a href="/wiki/Canningites" class="mw-redirect" title="Canningites">Canningites</a>. Conversely though, Canning had a reputation as a divisive man who alienated many.
</p><p>He was a dominant personality and often risked losing political allies for personal reasons. He once reduced Lord Liverpool to tears with a long satirical poem mocking Liverpool's attachment to his time as a colonel in the militia.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (March 2010)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Under_Secretary_of_State_for_Foreign_Affairs">Under Secretary of State for Foreign Affairs</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=George_Canning&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Under Secretary of State for Foreign Affairs">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>On 2 November 1795, Canning received his first ministerial post: <a href="/wiki/Parliamentary_Under_Secretary_of_State" class="mw-redirect" title="Parliamentary Under Secretary of State">Under Secretary of State</a> for Foreign Affairs. In this post he proved a strong supporter of Pitt, often taking his side in disputes with the <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Foreign_Affairs_(UK)" class="mw-redirect" title="Secretary of State for Foreign Affairs (UK)">Foreign Secretary</a>, <a href="/wiki/William_Wyndham_Grenville,_1st_Baron_Grenville" class="mw-redirect" title="William Wyndham Grenville, 1st Baron Grenville">Lord Grenville</a>. At the end of 1798 Canning responded to a resolution by <a href="/wiki/George_Tierney" title="George Tierney">George Tierney</a> MP for peace negotiations with France:
</p>
<blockquote><p>I for my part still conceive it to be the paramount duty of a British member of parliament to consider what is good for Great Britain&#160;... I do not envy that man's feelings, who can behold the sufferings of Switzerland, and who derives from that sight no idea of what is meant by the deliverance of Europe. I do not envy the feelings of that man, who can look without emotion at Italy – plundered, insulted, trampled upon, exhausted, covered with ridicule, and horror, and devastation – who can look at all this, and be at a loss to guess what is meant by the deliverance of Europe? As little do I envy the feelings of that man, who can view the peoples of the Netherlands driven into insurrection, and struggling for their freedom against the heavy hand of a merciless tyranny, without entertaining any suspicion of what may be the sense of the word deliverance. Does such a man contemplate Holland groaning under arbitrary oppressions and exactions? Does he turn his eyes to Spain trembling at the nod of a foreign master? And does the word deliverance still sound unintelligibly in his ear? Has he heard of the rescue and salvation of Naples, by the appearance and the triumphs of the British fleet? Does he know that the monarchy of Naples maintains its existence at the sword's point? And is his understanding, and his heart, still impenetrable to the sense and meaning of the deliverance of Europe?<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Pitt called this speech "one of the best ever heard on any occasion".<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He resigned his position at the Foreign Office on 1 April 1799.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="The_Anti-Jacobin"><i>The Anti-Jacobin</i></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=George_Canning&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: The Anti-Jacobin">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Canning was involved in the founding of the <i><a href="/wiki/Anti-Jacobin" title="Anti-Jacobin">Anti-Jacobin</a></i>, a newspaper which was published every Monday from 20 November 1797 to 9 July 1798. Its purpose was to support the government and condemn revolutionary doctrines through news and poetry, much of it written by Canning.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_2-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> Canning's poetry satirised and ridiculed Jacobin poetry.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Before the appearance of the <i>Anti-Jacobin</i> all the eloquence (except for Burke's) and all the wit and ridicule had been on the side of Fox and Sheridan. Canning and his friends changed this.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> A young Whig, <a href="/wiki/William_Lamb,_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne" title="William Lamb, 2nd Viscount Melbourne">William Lamb</a> (the future Lord Melbourne, Prime Minister) wrote an 'Epistle to the Editors of the <i>Anti-Jacobin'</i>, which attacked Canning:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><div class="poem">
<p>Who e'er ye are, all hail! – whether the skill<br />
Of youthful CANNING guides the ranc'rous quill;<br />
With powers mechanic far above his age,<br />
Adapts the paragraph and fills the page;<br />
Measures the column, mends what e'er's amiss,<br />
Rejects THAT letter, and accepts of THIS;<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
</div></blockquote>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Subsequent_offices">Subsequent offices</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=George_Canning&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Subsequent offices">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1799 Canning became a Commissioner of the Board of Control for India. Canning wrote on 16 April: "Here I am immersed in papers, of which I do not yet comprehend three words in succession; but I shall get at their meaning by degrees and at my leisure. No such hard work here as at my former office. No attendance but when I like it, when there are interesting letters received from India (as is now the case) or to be sent out there".<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Canning was appointed <a href="/wiki/Paymaster_of_the_Forces" title="Paymaster of the Forces">Paymaster of the Forces</a> (and therefore to the Privy Council as well) in 1800.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> In February 1801 Pitt resigned as Prime Minister due to the King's opposition to <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Emancipation" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholic Emancipation">Catholic Emancipation</a>. Canning, despite Pitt's advice to stay in office, loyally followed him into opposition. The following day, Canning wrote to Lady Malmesbury: "I resign <i>because Pitt</i> resigns. And that is all".<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Backbenches">Backbenches</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=George_Canning&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Backbenches">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Canning disliked being out of office, and wrote on to <a href="/wiki/John_Hookham_Frere" title="John Hookham Frere">John Hookham Frere</a> in summer 1801: "But the thought will obtrude itself now and then that I am not where I should be – <i>non-hoc pollicitus</i>."<sup id="cite_ref-Hinde1_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hinde1-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> He also claimed that Pitt had done "scrupulously and magnanimously <i>right</i> by everyone <i>but</i> me".<sup id="cite_ref-Hinde1_17-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hinde1-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> At the end of September 1801 Canning wrote to Frere, saying of Pitt: "I do love him, and reverence him as I should a Father – but a Father should not sacrifice me, with my good will. Most heartily I forgive him, But he has to answer to himself, and to the country for much mischief that he has done and much that is still to do."<sup id="cite_ref-Hinde1_17-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hinde1-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Pitt wished for Canning to enter <a href="/wiki/Henry_Addington,_1st_Viscount_Sidmouth" class="mw-redirect" title="Henry Addington, 1st Viscount Sidmouth">Henry Addington</a>'s government, a move which Canning looked on as a horrible dilemma but in the end, he turned the offer down.<sup id="cite_ref-Hinde1_17-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hinde1-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Canning opposed the preliminaries of the <a href="/wiki/Peace_of_Amiens" class="mw-redirect" title="Peace of Amiens">Peace of Amiens</a> signed on 1 October. He did not vote against it due to his personal devotion to Pitt.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> He wrote on 22 November: "I would risk my life to be assured of being able to act always <i>with P</i> in a manner satisfactory to my own feelings and sense of what is right, rather than have to seek that object in separation from him."<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> On 27 May 1802 in the Commons Canning requested that all grants of land in Trinidad (captured by Britain from Spain) should be rejected until Parliament had decided what to do with the island. The threat that it could be populated by slaves like other West Indian islands was real. Canning instead wanted it to have a military post and that it should be settled with ex-soldiers, free blacks and creoles, with the Native American population protected and helped. He also asserted that the island should be used to test the theory that better methods of cultivation in land would lessen the need for slaves. Addington acceded to Canning's demands and the Reverend William Leigh believed Canning had saved 750,000 lives.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At a dinner to celebrate Pitt's birthday in 1802, Canning wrote the song "The Pilot that Weathered the Storm", performed by a tenor from Drury Lane, <a href="/wiki/Charles_Dignum" title="Charles Dignum">Charles Dignum</a>:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><div class="poem">
<p>And oh! if again the rude whirlwind should rise,<br />
<span class="mw-poem-indented" style="display: inline-block; margin-inline-start: 1em;">The dawnings of peace should fresh darkness deform,</span><br />
The regrets of the good and the fears of the wise<br />
<span class="mw-poem-indented" style="display: inline-block; margin-inline-start: 1em;">Shall turn to the Pilot that weathered the Storm.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup></span>
</p>
</div></blockquote>
<p>In November Canning spoke out openly in support of Pitt in the Commons. One observer thought that Canning made incomparably the best speech and that his defence of Pitt's administration "one of the best things, either argumentatively as to matter, or critically and to manner and style" that he could ever remember.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> On 8 December Sheridan spoke out in defence of Addington and denied that Pitt was the only man who could save the country. Canning replied by criticising the Addington government's foreign policy and claimed that the House should recognise the greatness of the country and Pitt, who ought to be its leader. He argued against those, such as <a href="/wiki/William_Wilberforce" title="William Wilberforce">William Wilberforce</a>, who held that Britain could safely maintain a policy of isolation: "Let us consider the state of the world as it is, not as we fancy it ought to be. Let us not seek to hide from our own eyes&#160;... the real, imminent and awful danger which threatens us." Also, he objected to the notion that Britain could choose between greatness and happiness: "The choice is not in our power. We have&#160;... no refuge in littleness. We must maintain ourselves what we are, or cease to have a political existence worth preserving." Furthermore, he openly declared for Pitt and said: "Away with the cant of 'measures, not men., the idle supposition that it is the harness and not the horses that draw the chariot along." Kingdoms rise and fall due to what degree they are upheld "not by well-meaning endeavours&#160;... but by commanding, over-awing talents&#160;... retreat and withdraw as much as he will, he must not hope to efface the memory of his past services from the gratitude of his country; he cannot withdraw himself from the following of a nation; he must endure the attachment of a people whom he has saved."<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> In private Canning was fearful that if Pitt did not return to power, Fox would: "Sooner or later he <i>must</i> act or the country is gone."<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Canning approved of the declaration of war against France on 18 May 1803. Canning was angered by Pitt's desire not to proactively work to turn out the ministry but support the ministry when it adopted sound policies.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> However, in 1804, to Canning's delight, Pitt began to work against the Addington government. After Pitt delivered a stinging attack on the government's defence measures on 25 April, Canning launched his own attack on Addington, which made Addington furious. On 30 April <a href="/wiki/John_Scott,_1st_Earl_of_Eldon" title="John Scott, 1st Earl of Eldon">Lord Eldon</a>, the Lord Chancellor, asked Pitt to submit a new administration to the King.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Treasurer_of_the_Navy">Treasurer of the Navy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=George_Canning&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Treasurer of the Navy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Canning returned to office in 1804 with Pitt, becoming <a href="/wiki/Treasurer_of_the_Navy" title="Treasurer of the Navy">Treasurer of the Navy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> In 1805 he offered Pitt his resignation after Addington was given a seat in the Cabinet. He wrote to Lady Hester to say he felt humiliated that Addington was a minister "and I am – nothing. I cannot help it, I cannot face the House of Commons or walk the streets in this state of things, as I am".<sup id="cite_ref-Hinde2_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hinde2-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> After reading this letter Pitt summoned Canning to London for a meeting, where he told him that if he resigned it would open a permanent breach between the two of them as it would cast a slur on his conduct. He offered Canning the office of <a href="/wiki/Chief_Secretary_for_Ireland" title="Chief Secretary for Ireland">Chief Secretary for Ireland</a> but he refused on the grounds that this would look like he was being got out of the way. Canning eventually decided not to resign and wrote that "I am resolved to 'sink or swim' with Pitt, though he has tied himself to such sinking company. God forgive him".<sup id="cite_ref-Hinde2_28-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hinde2-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> Canning left office with the death of Pitt; he was not offered a place in Lord Grenville's administration.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_Secretary">Foreign Secretary</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=George_Canning&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Foreign Secretary">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:George_Canning.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/2/25/George_Canning.jpg" decoding="async" width="165" height="200" class="mw-file-element" data-file-width="165" data-file-height="200" /></a><figcaption>George Canning</figcaption></figure>
<p>Canning was appointed Foreign Secretary in the new government of the <a href="/wiki/William_Cavendish-Bentinck,_3rd_Duke_of_Portland" title="William Cavendish-Bentinck, 3rd Duke of Portland">Duke of Portland</a> in 1807. Given key responsibilities for the country's diplomacy in the <a href="/wiki/Napoleonic_Wars" title="Napoleonic Wars">Napoleonic Wars</a>, he was responsible for planning the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Copenhagen_(1807)" title="Battle of Copenhagen (1807)">attack on Copenhagen</a> in September 1807, much of which he undertook at his country estate, <a href="/wiki/South_Hill_Park" title="South Hill Park">South Hill Park</a> at <a href="/wiki/Easthampstead" title="Easthampstead">Easthampstead</a> in Berkshire.
</p><p>After the defeat of Prussia by the French, the neutrality of Denmark looked increasingly fragile. Canning was worried that Denmark might, under French pressure, become hostile to Britain.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> On the night of 21/22 July 1807 Canning received intelligence directly from <a href="/wiki/Sovetsk,_Kaliningrad_Oblast" title="Sovetsk, Kaliningrad Oblast">Tilsit</a> (where Napoleon and Tsar <a href="/wiki/Alexander_I_of_Russia" title="Alexander I of Russia">Alexander I of Russia</a> were negotiating a <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Tilsit" class="mw-redirect" title="Treaty of Tilsit">treaty</a>) which appeared "to rest on good authority" that Napoleon had proposed to the Tsar a great naval combination against Britain, of which Denmark and Portugal would be members.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 30 July a military force 25,000 strong set sail for Denmark, with Francis Jackson travelling the day after. Canning instructed Jackson that his overriding aim was to secure the possession of the Danish navy by offering the Danes a treaty of alliance and mutual defence whereby they would be given back their fleet at the end of the war. On 31 July Canning wrote to his wife: "The anxious interval between this day and the hearing the result of his [Jackson's] expedition will be long and painful indeed. Long, I mean, in <i>feeling</i>. In fact, it will be about a fortnight or three weeks&#160;... I think we have made success almost certain. But the measure is a bold one and if it fails – why we must be impeached I suppose – and dearest dear will have a box at the trial".<sup id="cite_ref-Hinde3_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hinde3-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> The day after he wrote that he had received a letter the previous night which provided an "account of the French being <i>actually</i> about to do that act of hostility, the possibility of which formed the groundwork of my Baltic plan. My fear was that the French might <i>not</i> be the aggressors – and then ours would have appeared a strong measure, fully justifiable I think and absolutely necessary, but without apparent necessity or justification. Now the aggression will justify us fully&#160;... I am therefore quite easy as to the morality and political wisdom of our plan".<sup id="cite_ref-Hinde3_32-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hinde3-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> Napoleon had on 31 July instructed his Foreign Minister, <a href="/wiki/Charles_Maurice_de_Talleyrand-P%C3%A9rigord" title="Charles Maurice de Talleyrand-Périgord">Talleyrand</a>, to inform the Danes that if they did not wish for Holstein to be invaded and occupied by <a href="/wiki/Charles_XIV_John_of_Sweden" class="mw-redirect" title="Charles XIV John of Sweden">Jean Bernadotte</a> they must prepare for war against Britain. Canning wrote to his wife on 1 August: "Now for the execution and I confess to my own love, I wake an hour or two earlier than I ought to, thinking of this execution. I could not sleep after asses' milk today, thought I was not in bed till 1/2 p.2". On 25 August he wrote to <a href="/wiki/George_Leveson-Gower,_1st_Duke_of_Sutherland" title="George Leveson-Gower, 1st Duke of Sutherland">Granville Leveson-Gower</a>: "The suspense is, as you may well imagine, agitating and painful in the extreme; but I have an undiminished confidence as to the result, either by force or by treaty. The latter however is so infinitely preferable to the former that the doubt whether it has been successful is of itself almost as anxious as if the whole depended on it alone".<sup id="cite_ref-Hinde3_32-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hinde3-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 2 September, after Jackson's negotiations proved unsuccessful, the British fleet began bombarding <a href="/wiki/Copenhagen" title="Copenhagen">Copenhagen</a> until when at 7&#160;pm on 5 September the Danes requested a truce. On 7 September the Danes agreed to hand over their navy (18 ships of the line, 15 frigates and 31 smaller ships) and naval stores and the British agreed to evacuate Zealand within six weeks. On 16 September Canning received the news with relief and excitement: "Did I not tell you we would save <a href="/wiki/Plumstead" title="Plumstead">Plumstead</a> from bombardment?" he wrote to the Reverend William Leigh. On 24 September he wrote to <a href="/wiki/George_Rose_(Treasurer_of_the_Navy)" class="mw-redirect" title="George Rose (Treasurer of the Navy)">George Rose</a>: "Nothing was ever more brilliant, more salutary or more effectual than the success [at Copenhagen]".<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> On 30 September he wrote <a href="/wiki/John_Parker,_1st_Earl_of_Morley" title="John Parker, 1st Earl of Morley">Lord Boringdon</a> that he hoped Copenhagen would "<i>stun</i> Russia into her sense again".<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Canning wrote to Gower on 2 October 1807: "We <i>are</i> hated throughout Europe and that hate must be cured by fear".<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> After the news of Russia's declaration of war against Britain reached London on 2 December, Canning wrote to Lord Boringdon two days later: "The Peace of Tilsit you see is come out. We did not want any more <i>case</i> for Copenhagen; but if we had, this gives it us".<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 3 February 1808, the opposition leader <a href="/wiki/George_Ponsonby" title="George Ponsonby">George Ponsonby</a> requested the publication of all information on the strength and battle-worthiness of the Danish fleet sent by the British envoy at Copenhagen. Canning replied with a speech nearly three hours long, described by <a href="/wiki/Henry_Temple,_3rd_Viscount_Palmerston" class="mw-redirect" title="Henry Temple, 3rd Viscount Palmerston">Lord Palmerston</a> as "so powerful that it gave a decisive turn to the debate". Lord Grey said his speech was "eloquent and powerful" but that he had never heard such "audacious misrepresentation" and "positive falsehood".<sup id="cite_ref-Hinde_(1973),_p._188_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hinde_(1973),_p._188-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> On 2 March the opposition moved a vote of censure over Copenhagen, defeated by 224 votes to 64 after Canning gave a speech, in the words of <a href="/wiki/Sylvester_Douglas,_1st_Baron_Glenbervie" title="Sylvester Douglas, 1st Baron Glenbervie">Lord Glenbervie</a>, so "very witty, very eloquent and very able".<sup id="cite_ref-Hinde_(1973),_p._188_37-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hinde_(1973),_p._188-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In November 1807, Canning oversaw the <a href="/wiki/Transfer_of_the_Portuguese_Court_to_Brazil" class="mw-redirect" title="Transfer of the Portuguese Court to Brazil">Portuguese royal family's flight</a> from Portugal to Brazil.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Duel_with_Castlereagh">Duel with Castlereagh</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=George_Canning&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Duel with Castlereagh">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1809 Canning entered into a series of disputes within the government that were to become famous. He argued with the <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_War_and_the_Colonies" title="Secretary of State for War and the Colonies">Secretary of State for War and the Colonies</a>, <a href="/wiki/Robert_Stewart,_Viscount_Castlereagh" title="Robert Stewart, Viscount Castlereagh">Lord Castlereagh</a>, over the deployment of troops that Canning had promised would be sent to Portugal but which Castlereagh sent to the Netherlands. The government became increasingly paralysed in disputes between the two men. Portland was in deteriorating health and gave no lead, until Canning threatened resignation unless Castlereagh was removed and replaced by <a href="/wiki/Richard_Wellesley,_1st_Marquess_Wellesley" title="Richard Wellesley, 1st Marquess Wellesley">Lord Wellesley</a>. Portland secretly agreed to make this change when it would be possible.
</p><p>Castlereagh discovered the deal in September 1809 and challenged Canning to a duel. Canning accepted the challenge and it was fought on 21 September 1809 on <a href="/wiki/Putney#Putney_Heath" title="Putney">Putney Heath</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Canning, who had never before fired a pistol, widely missed his mark. Castlereagh, who was regarded as one of the best shots of his day, wounded his opponent in the thigh. There was much outrage that two cabinet ministers had resorted to such a method. Shortly afterwards the ailing Portland resigned as Prime Minister, and Canning offered himself to <a href="/wiki/George_III_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="George III of the United Kingdom">George III</a> as a potential successor. The King appointed <a href="/wiki/Spencer_Perceval" title="Spencer Perceval">Spencer Perceval</a> instead, and Canning left office once more. He took consolation in the fact that Castlereagh also stood down.
</p><p>Upon <a href="/wiki/Assassination_of_Spencer_Perceval" title="Assassination of Spencer Perceval">Perceval's assassination</a> in 1812, the new Prime Minister, <a href="/wiki/Robert_Jenkinson,_2nd_Earl_of_Liverpool" title="Robert Jenkinson, 2nd Earl of Liverpool">Lord Liverpool</a>, offered Canning the position of Foreign Secretary once more. Canning refused, as he also wished to be <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_House_of_Commons" title="Leader of the House of Commons">Leader of the House of Commons</a> and was reluctant to serve in any government with Castlereagh.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> Castlereagh was appointed Foreign Secretary by Liverpool, replacing Wellesley who had resigned. Castlereagh remained in the post until his suicide in 1822, overseeing British participation in the final years of the <a href="/wiki/Napoleonic_Wars" title="Napoleonic Wars">Napoleonic Wars</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Congress_of_Vienna" title="Congress of Vienna">Congress of Vienna</a> which redrew the map of Europe.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Move_to_Burbage">Move to Burbage</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=George_Canning&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Move to Burbage">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In April 1811, George moved the family from Castle Hill House in <a href="/wiki/Hinckley" title="Hinckley">Hinckley</a> to the nearby village of <a href="/wiki/Burbage,_Leicestershire" title="Burbage, Leicestershire">Burbage</a>, where he took up residence in a house along Church Street, which in later years the house would become ‘Burbage Constitutional Club’.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Ambassador_to_Lisbon">Ambassador to Lisbon</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=George_Canning&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Ambassador to Lisbon">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1814 Canning became the British Ambassador to <a href="/wiki/Portugal" title="Portugal">Portugal</a>, recently liberated from the French by Wellington's forces. He returned to Britain the following year.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> He received several further offers of office from Liverpool.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="President_of_the_Board_of_Control">President of the Board of Control</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=George_Canning&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: President of the Board of Control">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>In 1816 he became <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_Board_of_Control" title="President of the Board of Control">President of the Board of Control</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Canning resigned from office once more in 1820, in opposition to the treatment of <a href="/wiki/Caroline_of_Brunswick" title="Caroline of Brunswick">Queen Caroline</a>, estranged wife of the new King <a href="/wiki/George_IV_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="George IV of the United Kingdom">George IV</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> Public opinion strongly supported the Queen and thereby Canning was strengthened.
</p><p>On 16 March 1821 Canning spoke in favour of William Plunket's Catholic Emancipation Bill.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> Liverpool wished to have Canning back in the Cabinet but the King was strongly hostile to him due to his actions over the Caroline affair. The King would only allow Canning back into the Cabinet if he did not have to deal personally with him. This required the office of Governor-General of India. After deliberating on whether to accept, Canning initially declined the offer but then accepted it.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> On 25 April he spoke in the Commons against <a href="/wiki/Lord_John_Russell" class="mw-redirect" title="Lord John Russell">Lord John Russell</a>'s motion for parliamentary reform and a few days later Canning moved for leave to introduce a measure of Catholic Emancipation (for lifting the exclusion of Catholics from the House of Lords). This passed the Commons but was rejected by the Lords.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_Secretary_and_Leader_of_the_House">Foreign Secretary and Leader of the House</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=George_Canning&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Foreign Secretary and Leader of the House">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:George_Canning_by_Richard_Evans.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1f/George_Canning_by_Richard_Evans.jpg/220px-George_Canning_by_Richard_Evans.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="339" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1f/George_Canning_by_Richard_Evans.jpg/330px-George_Canning_by_Richard_Evans.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1f/George_Canning_by_Richard_Evans.jpg/440px-George_Canning_by_Richard_Evans.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2400" data-file-height="3695" /></a><figcaption>Canning by Richard Evans, <i>circa</i> 1825</figcaption></figure>
<p>In August 1822, Castlereagh committed suicide. Instead of going to India, Canning succeeded him as both Foreign Secretary and Leader of the House of Commons.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> He gave support to the growing campaign for the abolition of slavery. Canning continued many of Castlereagh's foreign policies, such as the view that the powers of Europe (Russia, France, etc.) should not be allowed to meddle in the affairs of other states. This policy enhanced public opinion of Canning as a liberal. He also prevented the United States from opening trade with the British West Indies.
</p><p>Canning signed the <a href="/wiki/Anglo-Dutch_Treaty_of_1824" title="Anglo-Dutch Treaty of 1824">Anglo-Dutch Treaty of 1824</a> which resolved outstanding matters from <a href="/wiki/Anglo-Dutch_Treaty_of_1814" title="Anglo-Dutch Treaty of 1814">the previous treaty</a> intended to redistribute colonies formerly administered by the <a href="/wiki/Dutch_East_India_Company" title="Dutch East India Company">Dutch East India Company</a> in the East Indies, which had been seized during the <a href="/wiki/Napoleonic_Wars" title="Napoleonic Wars">Napoleonic Wars</a> by the <a href="/wiki/British_East_India_Company" class="mw-redirect" title="British East India Company">British East India Company</a> owing to Britain's position in the wake of the Napoleonic conquest of the Netherlands of recognising its holdings as hostile territory; the most violent conquest resulting from this policy was the 1811 <a href="/wiki/Invasion_of_Java_(1811)" title="Invasion of Java (1811)">invasion of Java</a> led by <a href="/wiki/Stamford_Raffles" title="Stamford Raffles">Stamford Raffles</a>. After the war, Raffles' extra-legal establishment of a trading post in <a href="/wiki/Singapore" title="Singapore">Singapore</a>, a vassal of the Dutch-backed <a href="/wiki/Johor_Sultanate" title="Johor Sultanate">Johor Sultanate</a>, formed the flashpoint for the 1824 renegotiation. This treaty's impact has lasted until the present day, with the established borders essentially being those between the modern nation-states of <a href="/wiki/Malaysia" title="Malaysia">Malaysia</a> and <a href="/wiki/Indonesia" title="Indonesia">Indonesia</a>; <a href="/wiki/Singapore" title="Singapore">Singapore</a> also was ruled directly by the EIC from that point on, with the former arrangement of equal control between <a href="/wiki/Sultan_Hussein_Shah" class="mw-redirect" title="Sultan Hussein Shah">Sultan Hussein Shah</a>, <a href="/wiki/Temenggong" title="Temenggong">Temenggong</a> Abdul Rahman and <a href="/wiki/Resident_of_Singapore" class="mw-redirect" title="Resident of Singapore">Resident of Singapore</a> <a href="/wiki/William_Farquhar" title="William Farquhar">William Farquhar</a> declared null and void in its aftermath.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Latin_America">Latin America</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=George_Canning&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Latin America">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>During his first period in the Foreign Office (1807–1809), Canning became deeply involved in the affairs of Spain, Portugal and Latin America. In his second term of office, he sought to prevent South America from coming into the French sphere of influence, and in this he was successful.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> He helped guarantee the independence of Brazil and the Spanish colonies, thereby acting in support of the <a href="/wiki/Monroe_Doctrine" title="Monroe Doctrine">Monroe Doctrine</a> and aiding British merchants to open new markets across South and Central America.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Britain had a strong interest in ensuring the demise of Spanish colonialism, and opening the newly independent Latin American colonies to its trade. The Latin Americans received a certain amount of unofficial aid – arms and volunteers – from outside, but no outside official help at any stage from Britain or any other power. Britain refused to aid Spain and opposed any outside intervention on behalf of Spain by other powers. Royal Navy veterans were a decisive factor in the struggle for independence of certain Latin American countries.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1825 Mexico, Argentina and Colombia were recognised by means of the ratification of commercial treaties with Britain. In November 1825 the first minister from a Latin American state, Colombia, was officially received in London. "Spanish America is free," Canning declared, "and if we do not mismanage our affairs she is English ... the New World established and if we do not throw it away, ours." Also in 1825, Portugal recognised Brazil (thanks to Canning's efforts, and in return for a preferential commercial treaty), less than three years after Brazil's declaration of independence.
</p><p>On 12 December 1826, in the House of Commons, Canning was given an opportunity to defend the policies he had adopted towards France, Spain and Spanish America, and declared: "I resolved that if France had Spain it should not be Spain with the Indies. I called the New World into existence to redress the balance of the Old."<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By granting recognition to Argentina, Colombia, Mexico and Brazil he brought these new states into the European system of trade and diplomacy, while blocking further colonization. Recognition was greeted with enthusiasm throughout Latin America. Canning was the first British Foreign Secretary to devote a large proportion of his time and energies to the affairs of Latin America (as well as to those of Spain and Portugal) and to foresee the important political and economic role the Latin American states would one day play in the world.
</p><p>At the Pan-American Centennial Conference of 1926 which took place in <a href="/wiki/Panama_City" title="Panama City">Panama City</a> and celebrated the centenary of the South American movement to Independence, it was declared that:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Great Britain lent to the liberty of Spanish America not only the support of its diplomacy, represented by Canning, but also an appreciable contingent of blood and it may be asserted that there was no battlefield in the War of Independence in which British blood was not shed.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>In 1826 he was elected a <a href="/wiki/Fellow_of_the_Royal_Society" title="Fellow of the Royal Society">Fellow of the Royal Society</a>.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Minister">Prime Minister<span class="anchor" id="Premiership"></span></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=George_Canning&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Prime Minister">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Canningite_government,_1827%E2%80%931828" title="Canningite government, 1827–1828">Canningite government, 1827–1828</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Post-Medieval_Medal_(FindID_742284).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/Post-Medieval_Medal_%28FindID_742284%29.jpg/220px-Post-Medieval_Medal_%28FindID_742284%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="105" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/Post-Medieval_Medal_%28FindID_742284%29.jpg/330px-Post-Medieval_Medal_%28FindID_742284%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/Post-Medieval_Medal_%28FindID_742284%29.jpg/440px-Post-Medieval_Medal_%28FindID_742284%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4956" data-file-height="2362" /></a><figcaption>A worn copper-alloy medal depicting Canning</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1827 Liverpool was forced to stand down as Prime Minister after suffering a severe stroke (he died the following year). Canning, as Liverpool's right-hand man, was chosen by George IV to succeed him, in preference to the <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington" title="Arthur Wellesley, 1st Duke of Wellington">Duke of Wellington</a> or Sir <a href="/wiki/Robert_Peel" title="Robert Peel">Robert Peel</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> Neither man agreed to serve under Canning, and they were followed by five other members of Liverpool's Cabinet and 40 junior members of the government. The Tory party was now heavily split between the "High Tories" (or <a href="/wiki/Ultra-Tories" title="Ultra-Tories">"Ultras"</a>, nicknamed after the <a href="/wiki/Ultra-royalist" title="Ultra-royalist">contemporary party</a> in France) and the moderates supporting Canning, often called "<a href="/wiki/Canningites" class="mw-redirect" title="Canningites">Canningites</a>". Consequently Canning found it difficult to form a government, and chose to invite a number of Whigs to join his Cabinet, including <a href="/wiki/Henry_Petty-Fitzmaurice,_3rd_Marquess_of_Lansdowne" title="Henry Petty-Fitzmaurice, 3rd Marquess of Lansdowne">Lord Lansdowne</a>. The government agreed not to discuss the difficult question of <a href="/wiki/Reform_Act_1832" title="Reform Act 1832">parliamentary reform</a>, which Canning opposed but the Whigs supported.
</p><p>However, Canning's health by this time was in steep decline: at the funeral of <a href="/wiki/Frederick,_Duke_of_York" class="mw-redirect" title="Frederick, Duke of York">Frederick, Duke of York</a>, which was held during a January night at <a href="/wiki/St_George%27s_Chapel,_Windsor_Castle" title="St George&#39;s Chapel, Windsor Castle">St George's Chapel, Windsor Castle</a>, he became so ill that it was thought he might not recover.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> He died from <a href="/wiki/Tuberculosis" title="Tuberculosis">tuberculosis</a> later that year, on 8 August 1827, in the room where <a href="/wiki/Charles_James_Fox" title="Charles James Fox">Charles James Fox</a> had died 21 years earlier. Canning's total period in office was 119 days, the shortest of any <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Prime Minister of the United Kingdom">Prime Minister of the United Kingdom</a> who had formed a government until <a href="/wiki/Liz_Truss" title="Liz Truss">Liz Truss</a>, forced to resign after fifty days in 2022.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> His last words were "Spain and Portugal."<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> Canning is buried in <a href="/wiki/Westminster_Abbey" title="Westminster Abbey">Westminster Abbey</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=George_Canning&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Statue_of_George_Canning,_Parliament_Square,_London.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/Statue_of_George_Canning%2C_Parliament_Square%2C_London.jpg/170px-Statue_of_George_Canning%2C_Parliament_Square%2C_London.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="255" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/Statue_of_George_Canning%2C_Parliament_Square%2C_London.jpg/255px-Statue_of_George_Canning%2C_Parliament_Square%2C_London.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/Statue_of_George_Canning%2C_Parliament_Square%2C_London.jpg/340px-Statue_of_George_Canning%2C_Parliament_Square%2C_London.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3471" data-file-height="5205" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Statue_of_George_Canning,_Parliament_Square" title="Statue of George Canning, Parliament Square">Statue of Canning</a> in <a href="/wiki/Parliament_Square" title="Parliament Square">Parliament Square</a>, London, by Sir <a href="/wiki/Richard_Westmacott" title="Richard Westmacott">Richard Westmacott</a>. Erected in 1832.<sup id="cite_ref-BHO45180_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BHO45180-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>Canning has come to be regarded as a "lost leader", with much speculation about what his legacy could have been had he lived. His government of Tories and Whigs continued for a few months under <a href="/wiki/Frederick_John_Robinson,_1st_Viscount_Goderich" class="mw-redirect" title="Frederick John Robinson, 1st Viscount Goderich">Lord Goderich</a> but fell apart in early 1828. It was succeeded by a government under the <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington" title="Arthur Wellesley, 1st Duke of Wellington">Duke of Wellington</a>, which initially included some <a href="/wiki/Canningites" class="mw-redirect" title="Canningites">Canningites</a> but soon became mostly "High Tory" when many of the Canningites drifted over to the Whigs. Wellington's administration would soon go down in defeat as well. Some historians have seen the revival of the Tories from the 1830s onwards, in the form of the <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_(UK)" title="Conservative Party (UK)">Conservative Party</a>, as the overcoming of the divisions of 1827. What would have been the course of events had Canning lived is highly speculative.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:George_Canning_House_2021.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/George_Canning_House_2021.jpg/220px-George_Canning_House_2021.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="293" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/George_Canning_House_2021.jpg/330px-George_Canning_House_2021.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/George_Canning_House_2021.jpg/440px-George_Canning_House_2021.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3120" data-file-height="4160" /></a><figcaption>Blue plaque at George Canning's home</figcaption></figure>
<p>Rory Muir has described Canning as "the most brilliant and colourful minister, and certainly the greatest orator in the government at a time when oratory was still politically important. He was a man of biting wit and invective, with immense confidence in his own ability, who often inspired either great friendship or deep dislike and distrust&#160;... he was a passionate, active, committed man who poured his energy into whatever he undertook. This was his strength and also his weakness&#160;... the government's ablest minister".<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Charles_Greville_(diarist)" title="Charles Greville (diarist)">Greville</a> recorded of Canning on the day after his death:
</p>
<blockquote><p>He wrote very fast, but not fast enough for his mind, composing much quicker than he could commit his ideas to paper. He could not bear to dictate, because nobody could write fast enough for him; but on one occasion, when he had gout in his hand and could not write, he stood by the fire and dictated at the same time a despatch on Greek affairs to <a href="/wiki/Lord_George_Bentinck" title="Lord George Bentinck">George Bentinck</a> and one on South American politics to <a href="/wiki/Charles_Ellis,_6th_Baron_Howard_de_Walden" title="Charles Ellis, 6th Baron Howard de Walden">Howard de Walden</a>, each writing as fast as he could, while he turned from one to the other without hesitation or embarrassment.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Canning was the arch-enemy of the <a href="/wiki/Concert_of_Europe" title="Concert of Europe">Concert of Europe</a> system set up by the conservative powers at the Congress of Vienna in 1815. Hayes says that in terms of foreign affairs:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>His most important achievement was the destruction of the system of the neo-Holy Alliance which, if unchallenged, must have dominated Europe. Canning realized it was not enough for Britain to boycott conferences and congresses; it was essential to persuade the Powers that their interests could not be advanced by a system of intervention based upon principles of legitimacy, <a href="/wiki/Anti-nationalism" class="mw-redirect" title="Anti-nationalism">anti-nationalism</a> and hostility to revolution.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Temperley summarises his policies, which formed the basis of British foreign policy for decades:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>non-intervention; no European police system; every nation for itself, and God for us all; balance of power; respect for facts, not for abstract theories; respect for treaty rights, but caution in extending them&#160;... a republic is as good a member of the comity of nations as a monarch. "England not Europe". "Our foreign policy cannot be conducted against the will of the nation." "Europe's domain extends to the shores of the Atlantic, England's begins there."<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>The Anglo-Dutch Treaty of 1824, negotiated by himself and <a href="/wiki/Charles_Williams-Wynn_(1775%E2%80%931850)" title="Charles Williams-Wynn (1775–1850)">Charles Williams-Wynn</a>, is responsible for the borders of the modern nation-states of Singapore, Malaysia and Indonesia. 
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Places_named_after_Canning">Places named after Canning</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=George_Canning&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Places named after Canning">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Britain">Britain</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=George_Canning&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Britain">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<ul><li>The <a href="/wiki/Canning_Club" title="Canning Club">Canning Club</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Gentlemen%27s_club" title="Gentlemen&#39;s club">gentlemen's club</a> in <a href="/wiki/Central_London" title="Central London">central London</a>. Founded in 1911 as the Argentine Club for <a href="/wiki/Expatriate" title="Expatriate">expatriate</a> businessmen, it was renamed in 1948 as the club extended its remit to the rest of Latin America, in honour of Canning's strong ties to the region. The club currently shares the premises of the <a href="/wiki/Naval_and_Military_Club" title="Naval and Military Club">Naval and Military Club</a> in <a href="/wiki/St._James%27s_Square" class="mw-redirect" title="St. James&#39;s Square">St. James's Square</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Canning_House" title="Canning House">Canning House</a> in <a href="/wiki/Belgravia" title="Belgravia">Belgravia</a> is the seat of the Hispanic and Luso Brazilian Council. It houses a research library and is used for a range of cultural and educational events.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>There are pubs (<a href="/wiki/Public_house" class="mw-redirect" title="Public house">public houses</a>) named after him. One in <a href="/wiki/Brixton" title="Brixton">Brixton</a>, on the corner of Effra Road and Brixton Water Lane, bore his name until it became the Hobgoblin in the late 1990s and the Hootenanny in 2008. Another in <a href="/wiki/Camberwell" title="Camberwell">Camberwell</a>, on Grove Lane near Denmark Hill station, is still called the George Canning.</li>
<li>Canning Circus is an area at the top of Zion Hill in <a href="/wiki/Nottingham" title="Nottingham">Nottingham</a>. <a href="/wiki/Canning_Terrace" title="Canning Terrace">Canning Terrace</a> was erected as almshouses and a gatehouse to the adjacent cemetery.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Canning,_Liverpool" title="Canning, Liverpool">Canning</a> is a district of Liverpool also known as the Georgian Quarter and includes Canning Street. Canning Place in <a href="/wiki/Liverpool_One" title="Liverpool One">Liverpool One</a> was the site of the <a href="/wiki/Liverpool_Sailors%27_Home" title="Liverpool Sailors&#39; Home">Liverpool Sailors' Home</a>.  <a href="/wiki/Canning_Dock" title="Canning Dock">Canning Dock</a> and <a href="/wiki/Canning_Half_Tide_Dock" title="Canning Half Tide Dock">Canning Half Tide Dock</a> on the <a href="/wiki/River_Mersey" title="River Mersey">River Mersey</a> are also named after Canning.</li>
<li>Canning Street in Belfast is named after George Canning.</li>
<li>Canning Crescent, <a href="/wiki/Oxford" title="Oxford">Oxford</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Canning's View, <a href="/wiki/Cliveden" title="Cliveden">Cliveden</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Australia">Australia</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=George_Canning&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Australia">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<ul><li>The <a href="/wiki/Canning_River,_Western_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Canning River, Western Australia">Canning River</a> in Western Australia, which flows into the <a href="/wiki/Swan_River,_Western_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Swan River, Western Australia">Swan River</a> south of <a href="/wiki/Perth" title="Perth">Perth</a>. A number of districts on its banks are named after the river, rather than Canning himself; for example <a href="/wiki/Cannington,_Western_Australia" title="Cannington, Western Australia">Cannington</a> and <a href="/wiki/Canning_Vale" class="mw-redirect" title="Canning Vale">Canning Vale</a>.</li>
<li>In <a href="/wiki/Queensland" title="Queensland">Queensland</a>, <a href="/wiki/Canning_Creek,_Queensland" title="Canning Creek, Queensland">Canning Creek</a> (the watercourse, the locality and the pastoral run) were all named after him.<sup id="cite_ref-qpnl2_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-qpnl2-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Elsewhere in Australia, there is a street in <a href="/wiki/Carlton_North" title="Carlton North">Carlton North</a> named after him.</li></ul>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Canada">Canada</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=George_Canning&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Canada">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<ul><li>The village of <a href="/wiki/Canning,_Nova_Scotia" title="Canning, Nova Scotia">Canning</a> in the Annapolis Valley of Nova Scotia</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Cannington,_Ontario" title="Cannington, Ontario">Cannington, Ontario</a>, is a small village in Brock Township.</li></ul>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="South_America">South America</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=George_Canning&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: South America">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<ul><li>In Buenos Aires, Argentina, <a href="/wiki/Scalabrini_Ortiz_Avenue" class="mw-redirect" title="Scalabrini Ortiz Avenue">a street has been on-and-off named after Canning</a> since 1893, changing away from the name in 1985. There is also a neighbourhood in southwestern Buenos Aires named after Canning, it started as a station and today is occupied by many 'country clubs' where richer people live.</li>
<li>In Montevideo, Uruguay, there is a street named Jorge Canning, the location of the British Residence.</li>
<li>There is also a street named after him in the district of <a href="/wiki/Ipanema" title="Ipanema">Ipanema</a>, Rio de Janeiro, Brazil.</li>
<li>In <a href="/wiki/Santiago" title="Santiago">Santiago</a>, <a href="/wiki/Chile" title="Chile">Chile</a> there are two streets called Jorge (Spanish for George) Canning, one in the commune of San Joaquin and a smaller one in the commune of Ñuñoa.</li></ul>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Greece">Greece</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=George_Canning&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Greece">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<ul><li>A square in downtown <a href="/wiki/Athens" title="Athens">Athens</a>, Greece, is named after Canning (Πλατεία Κάνιγγος, <i>Plateía Kánningos</i>, Canning Square), in appreciation of his supportive stance toward the <a href="/wiki/Greek_War_of_Independence" title="Greek War of Independence">Greek War of Independence</a> (1821–1830).</li></ul>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Places_named_after_other_Cannings">Places named after other Cannings</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=George_Canning&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Places named after other Cannings">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Fort_Canning" class="mw-redirect" title="Fort Canning">Fort Canning</a>, a hill in Singapore, is actually named after Canning's son <a href="/wiki/Charles_Canning,_1st_Earl_Canning" title="Charles Canning, 1st Earl Canning">Viscount Charles Canning</a>, although many mistakenly believe that it was named after George Canning himself. The hill was previously known simply as <i>Government Hill</i> and earlier as <i>Bukit Larangan</i> (<a href="/wiki/Malay_language" title="Malay language">Malay</a> for Forbidden Hill) as it was once the seat for <a href="/wiki/Sultan_of_Johor" title="Sultan of Johor">Malay royalty</a>. Having served as an administrative centre for the British colonial administration in Singapore, it was then converted into a fort during the <a href="/wiki/First_World_War" class="mw-redirect" title="First World War">First World War</a> but was not used until the <a href="/wiki/Second_World_War" class="mw-redirect" title="Second World War">Second World War</a>. It is currently one of Singapore's oldest <a href="/wiki/Urban_park" title="Urban park">urban parks</a>.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Canning_Town" title="Canning Town">Canning Town</a> in London is often thought of as being named after George Canning, but was in fact named after his son <a href="/wiki/Charles_Canning,_1st_Earl_Canning" title="Charles Canning, 1st Earl Canning">Charles Canning, 1st Earl Canning</a>, Governor-General of India during the <a href="/wiki/Indian_Mutiny" class="mw-redirect" title="Indian Mutiny">Indian Mutiny</a>.</li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Family">Family</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=George_Canning&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Family">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Canning married <a href="/wiki/Joan_Canning,_1st_Viscountess_Canning" title="Joan Canning, 1st Viscountess Canning">Joan Scott</a> (later <a href="/wiki/Earl_Canning" title="Earl Canning">1st Viscountess Canning</a>) (1776–1837) on 8 July 1800, with John Hookham Frere and William Pitt the Younger as witnesses.
</p><p>George and Joan Canning had four children:
</p>
<ul><li>George Charles Canning (1801–1820), died from <a href="/wiki/Tuberculosis" title="Tuberculosis">consumption</a></li>
<li>William Pitt Canning (1802–1828), died from drowning in <a href="/wiki/Madeira" title="Madeira">Madeira</a>, Portugal</li>
<li>Harriet Canning (1804–1876), married <a href="/wiki/Ulick_de_Burgh,_1st_Marquess_of_Clanricarde" title="Ulick de Burgh, 1st Marquess of Clanricarde">Ulick de Burgh, 1st Marquess of Clanricarde</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Charles_John_Canning,_1st_Earl_Canning" class="mw-redirect" title="Charles John Canning, 1st Earl Canning">Charles John Canning</a> (later 2nd Viscount Canning and 1st Earl Canning) (1812–1862)</li></ul>
<h2><span id="Canning.27s_Government.2C_April.E2.80.93August_1827"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Canning's_Government,_April–August_1827">Canning's Government, April–August 1827</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=George_Canning&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Canning&#039;s Government, April–August 1827">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<ul><li>George Canning – <a href="/wiki/First_Lord_of_the_Treasury" title="First Lord of the Treasury">First Lord of the Treasury</a>, <a href="/wiki/Chancellor_of_the_Exchequer" title="Chancellor of the Exchequer">Chancellor of the Exchequer</a> and <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_House_of_Commons" title="Leader of the House of Commons">Leader of the House of Commons</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/John_Copley,_1st_Baron_Lyndhurst" title="John Copley, 1st Baron Lyndhurst">Lord Lyndhurst</a> – <a href="/wiki/Lord_Chancellor" title="Lord Chancellor">Lord Chancellor</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Dudley_Ryder,_1st_Earl_of_Harrowby" title="Dudley Ryder, 1st Earl of Harrowby">Lord Harrowby</a> – <a href="/wiki/Lord_President_of_the_Council" title="Lord President of the Council">Lord President of the Council</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/William_Bentinck,_4th_Duke_of_Portland" title="William Bentinck, 4th Duke of Portland">The Duke of Portland</a> – <a href="/wiki/Lord_Privy_Seal" title="Lord Privy Seal">Lord Privy Seal</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/William_Sturges_Bourne" title="William Sturges Bourne">William Sturges Bourne</a> – <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_the_Home_Department" class="mw-redirect" title="Secretary of State for the Home Department">Secretary of State for the Home Department</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/John_William_Ward,_1st_Earl_of_Dudley" class="mw-redirect" title="John William Ward, 1st Earl of Dudley">Lord Dudley</a> – <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Foreign_Affairs_(UK)" class="mw-redirect" title="Secretary of State for Foreign Affairs (UK)">Secretary of State for Foreign Affairs</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Frederick_John_Robinson,_1st_Viscount_Goderich" class="mw-redirect" title="Frederick John Robinson, 1st Viscount Goderich">Lord Goderich</a> – <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_War_and_the_Colonies" title="Secretary of State for War and the Colonies">Secretary of State for War and the Colonies</a> and <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_House_of_Lords" title="Leader of the House of Lords">Leader of the House of Lords</a></li>
